Observed cost per accepted task divides the total charge for all initiated trials by their accepted count; its population counterpart is \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) from equation 1.2, with \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\) as the attempt cost and \(\Pr(\text{Acc}_\pi=1)\) as \(\AcceptanceProbability\). Because failed trials are charged to accepted ones, the ratio never falls below the mean cost of the successful trials alone, and a cohort with no accepted tasks reports its spend and the zero count.

Candidate availability under pass@\IndependentAttemptCount priced breadth in candidates under the most favorable assumptions: independent, isolated candidates with constant pass probability \(\AttemptPassProbability>0\) and a checker that decides the stipulated criterion exactly. A parallel pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) always spends \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates and reaches availability \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k=1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\). Sequential generation that stops at the first pass reaches the same \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k\) after an expected \(\mathbb{E}[\SequentialCandidateCount_k]=\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k/\AttemptPassProbability\) candidates, and never more than \(\IndependentAttemptCount\). Charging each candidate its generation cost \(\CandidateGenerationCost\) and its checking cost \(\CandidateCheckingCost\), in dollars, and charging every initiated trial against the accepted ones turns those counts into costs per accepted task:

\[\BatchCostPerAcceptedTask=\frac{\IndependentAttemptCount(\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)}{\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k},\qquad \SequentialCostPerAcceptedTask=\frac{\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost}{\AttemptPassProbability}. \tag{6}\]

Pricing generation and checking separately exposes a third policy that candidate counts hide. Generating all candidates first and stopping only the checking early costs \(\IndependentAttemptCount \CandidateGenerationCost+(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k/\AttemptPassProbability)\CandidateCheckingCost\) per trial in expectation, because generation already performed stays paid for even when checking stops. Choosing a generator under sequential stopping therefore reduces to comparing \((\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)/\AttemptPassProbability\) across generators rather than choosing \(\IndependentAttemptCount\), and when checking dominates, raising \(\AttemptPassProbability\) upstream wins because every failed attempt pays for a check. The formulas in equation 6 omit latency constraints, checker errors, task heterogeneity, and adaptive feedback.

↩︎ The trade-off between spending a fixed deliberation budget on generating candidates and on checking them is established in Balancing pool size against checking cost.

Napkin Math 13.3: Pricing generation and checking separately
Problem: At the same candidate cap, how much does stopping generation at the first pass save per accepted task once generation and checking carry separate dollar prices?

Assumptions: Use a fixed-task pass probability of 40 percent, at most 3 isolated candidates, generation cost $0.03, and exact checking cost $0.01 per candidate, with no other costs.

Math: Both policies return a passing candidate with probability 78.4 percent. Full batch generation and checking cost $0.12 per initiated trial. Sequential generation and checking start 1.96 candidates on average and cost $0.0784. Either policy’s all-fail path incurs the full batch cost. Charging failed trials to accepted ones gives approximately $0.153 for the batch policy vs. $0.10 for sequential early stopping.

Systems insight: Under sequential stopping, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask=(\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)/\AttemptPassProbability\) whatever the cap, so raising the cap buys acceptance probability at no increase in cost per accepted task, while the full batch here pays a 53 percent premium for the same acceptance. Generating all candidates and stopping only the checks falls between the two, since it pays the batch’s full generation cost but only the sequential policy’s expected checking cost. With generation and checking prices held fixed, the one knob that lowers \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) is the single-candidate pass probability. Doubling \(\AttemptPassProbability\) halves it, to $0.05. An imperfect selector, a deadline, or a shared environment changes these formulas and is measured as part of the policy.

Checkpoint 13.3: Statistical evidence

These metrics quantify outcomes under a declared experiment, and for the worked comparison they return an offline verdict and stop there. candidate_v2 accepted more paired tasks than baseline_v1, yet its paired interval does not exclude a loss larger than the declared tolerance, so the offline evidence establishes neither noninferiority nor superiority. A powered next sample needs its gain, discordance, and tolerance fixed before any data arrive. What the metrics cannot report is why an individual trial failed. A lower acceptance rate could arise from the model, missing context, an observation boundary, or a slow dependency, and separating those causes takes execution evidence and controlled experiments rather than more aggregate counts.

13.6 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Even an apparently thorough evaluation report can answer the wrong engineering question. The failure modes below break the link between the evidence an offline evaluation collects and the claim it is used to support, and each exacts a measurable cost in reliability, sample efficiency, or grading accuracy.

Fallacy: An agent that succeeds on 90 percent of runs is a reliable agent.

Single-run success rates evaluate an agent executing an isolated task once. Real agentic workloads, however, recur, and the probability of passing every recurrence falls geometrically with their number (section 13.5.4). When an agent achieves a 90 percent single-run acceptance rate, a task that recurs 8 times succeeds on every recurrence only 43 percent of the time. Sustaining 90 percent end-to-end consistency across all 8 invocations demands an individual single-run acceptance rate of 98.7 percent. Evaluation harnesses should report pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) when the claim concerns repeated executions of the same task without human intervention, and reserve selected acceptance metrics for workflows where an automated checker can safely discard and retry intermediate failures.

Pitfall: Comparing two systems by whether their separate confidence intervals overlap.

Checking whether two marginal confidence intervals overlap is an overly conservative and statistically flawed criterion for comparing candidate releases. Separate Wilson score intervals quantify candidate and baseline success rates in isolation, but deployment decisions hinge on the distribution of their paired differences across identical task inputs (section 13.5.3). Positive within-task covariance reduces the variance of the difference. In the benchmark comparison, exploiting that covariance retains only about 40 percent of the variance present in two independent arms. Evaluating the systems as unpaired cohorts forces teams to run roughly 2.5× as many tasks to achieve identical statistical power. Teams should preserve task-level pairing throughout the evaluation harness, specify the required noninferiority margin before running the benchmark, and decide releases based on the lower bound of the paired confidence interval.

Fallacy: A grader that is isolated from the agent and agrees with a second judge is a correct grader.

Isolating a grader from the agent and limiting submitted-code execution protect measurement integrity within those enforced boundaries, while inter-rater agreement confirms that two evaluators behave consistently. However, consistent agreement does not guarantee ground-truth correctness (section 13.4). Two automated judges that each approve 90 percent of submissions without analyzing the execution trace will agree on 82 percent of evaluations purely by chance, yielding a Cohen’s \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) of 0. High rater consensus often conceals shared systematic biases or overly lenient acceptance thresholds. Evaluation pipelines must calibrate automated judges against a curated test suite of known, independently verified agent failures and explicitly track the grader’s false-acceptance rate.

Pitfall: Tuning a configuration against the held-out suite that will judge it.

Every prompt, tool definition, or architecture change chosen because it fixed a held-out failure moves information from the suite into the configuration (section 13.3.5). After enough such rounds the held-out score measures fit to that suite rather than performance on the task population it was sampled from, and no sandbox reset or similarity filter detects the drift, because nothing leaked through the environment (Dwork et al. 2015). A paired interval computed on that suite then understates the uncertainty of the claim it supports, since its tasks were no longer chosen independently of the configuration. Teams should develop against a separate development suite, retire a held-out suite into development use once it has steered decisions, and reserve a fresh sample for the comparison that decides between configurations.

Fallacy: The configuration with the higher accuracy is the better configuration.

Accuracy alone ranks a policy that retries until something passes above one that answers once, even when the first spends far more per accepted task, which is why leaderboards that rank accuracy alone reward needlessly costly agent designs (Kapoor et al. 2024). Two policies can even reach the same acceptance probability at different costs. At the same candidate cap, the full batch of notebook 13.3 pays a 53 percent premium per accepted task over sequential stopping (section 13.5.5). Comparisons should fix the task distribution, criteria, and resource envelope before any trial runs, charge every failed attempt to the accepted tasks, and report budgeted acceptance and cost per accepted task alongside accuracy.

13.7 Summary

An agent run concludes by emitting an artifact and an execution trace, but evaluation determines what that run actually proves. The discipline developed across this chapter holds every claim about a configuration accountable to the evidence that generated it. A passing unit assertion verifies only that isolated check rather than end-to-end task completion. A successful run in a test fixture validates behavior only within that harness. Agreement among automated raters reflects consistency rather than ground truth. An accuracy reported without its cost ranks designs partly by how much they were allowed to spend. When observational evidence is incomplete or ambiguous, the verdict is recorded as pending, unknown, or inconclusive rather than granted as an unearned pass. The worked candidate shows the consequence. It accepted more paired tasks than the baseline, yet its paired interval cannot rule out a loss larger than the declared tolerance, so the offline comparison holds it back. Such a hold is the evaluation harness working as intended, because a comparison sized to its noise can be audited and repeated with a powered sample, whereas an uncalibrated scorecard conceals the risk it was meant to measure.

The quantitative decisions developed across this chapter rely on the mathematical relationships summarized in table 7. Each formulation either sizes the empirical evidence required to support a claim about a configuration or prices the acceptance that evidence reports.

Key Takeaways: Match every evaluation claim to its evidence
  • Acceptance is a contract, not a completion claim: A trial counts as successful only when recorded execution evidence satisfies pre-declared criteria across functional outcomes, operational processes, resource budgets, and residual side effects. Unresolved, crashed, or timed-out runs must remain in the evaluation denominator. Dropping inconclusive tasks artificially alters the target population and deceptively deflates the reported effective cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)) without reducing actual system expenditure.
  • A grader establishes only what it covers: Isolated test fixtures and secured grading boundaries prevent an untrusted agent artifact from manipulating its own evaluation harness, yet any automated check validates only the specific invariants it exercises. Furthermore, strong agreement between automated or human judges reflects rater consistency rather than ground-truth correctness. Detecting false acceptances requires calibrating automated graders against verified reference evaluations.
  • Tasks carry the evidence; pairing multiplies it: Once repeats exceed the variance ratio \(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2/\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2\), the between-task component dominates. Only new tasks reduce that component, but fixture cost still enters the allocation; repeats continue to reduce the run component. Pairing preserves the configurations’ within-task dependence. Positive covariance reduces the difference variance, as in the worked comparison.
  • The deployment policy picks the metric: Availability across a candidate pool (\(\operatorname{pass@}\IndependentAttemptCount\)) fits workflows where an automated checker can retry failed attempts cheaply, whereas recurrence consistency (\(\operatorname{pass}^\IndependentAttemptCount\)) is mandatory when an agent must succeed reliably on every invocation without external intervention. For example, a single-run success rate of 90 percent achieves all 8 consecutive recurrences only 43 percent of the time.
  • Acceptance has a price that belongs in the comparison: Policies are compared under a fixed task distribution, criteria, and resource envelope, with every failed attempt charged to the accepted tasks. Under sequential selection the cost per accepted task depends on the single-candidate pass probability rather than on the candidate cap, so the lever that lowers it is a better generator, not more retries.

The statistical half of causal and statistical release evidence (principle 12) requires an evaluation protocol to state its task criteria, the population its tasks were drawn from, and the uncertainty of every comparison before it collects data. Its causal half, which ties a claim to retained traces and controlled interventions, belongs with the exposure policy for a release in Operating Agents.

13.8 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The statistical foundations of agent evaluation predate electronic computing, emerging from industrial quality control and experimental design. Wilson derived the score interval in 1927 to bound binomial success probabilities reliably even in small sample regimes where normal approximations fail (Wilson 1927). Two decades later, McNemar formulated his matched-pairs test to compare binary outcomes across identical subjects, establishing a rigorous basis for head-to-head model comparisons on fixed benchmark sets without assuming treatment independence (McNemar 1947). For qualitative metrics, Cohen introduced \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) to quantify inter-annotator agreement beyond chance (Cohen 1960), while Efron later introduced the non-parametric bootstrap to estimate sampling variance and confidence intervals for arbitrary statistics lacking closed-form analytical distributions (Efron 1979). In 2001, Brown, Cai, and DasGupta demonstrated that the standard Wald interval oscillates erratically in coverage and systematically fails near boundary proportions, even at sample sizes of several hundred. For that reason, this chapter adopts Wilson’s score interval as the engineering default for small-sample agent evaluation suites (Brown et al. 2001).

Wilson, Edwin B. 1927. “Probable Inference, the Law of Succession, and Statistical Inference.” Journal of the American Statistical Association 22 (158): 209–12. https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1927.10502953.
McNemar, Quinn. 1947. “Note on the Sampling Error of the Difference Between Correlated Proportions or Percentages.” Psychometrika 12 (2): 153–57. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02295996.
Cohen, Jacob. 1960. “A Coefficient of Agreement for Nominal Scales.” Educational and Psychological Measurement 20 (1): 37–46. https://doi.org/10.1177/001316446002000104.
Efron, Bradley. 1979. “Bootstrap Methods: Another Look at the Jackknife.” The Annals of Statistics 7 (1): 1–26. https://doi.org/10.1214/aos/1176344552.
Recht, Benjamin, Rebecca Roelofs, Ludwig Schmidt, and Vaishaal Shankar. 2019. “Do ImageNet Classifiers Generalize to ImageNet?” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5389–400.

Evaluation methodologies in machine learning arrived at these same conclusions through hard-won practical failures. Dwork et al. (2015) demonstrated that iteratively consulting a held-out evaluation dataset to guide modeling decisions leaks information and causes adaptive overfitting, establishing formal bounds through differential privacy to preserve dataset reuse. Shortly thereafter, Recht et al. (2019) collected new, independent test sets for ImageNet and observed pervasive accuracy degradation across leading architectures, attributing the drops to subtle shifts between the original and replicated test distributions rather than to adaptive overfitting of the original benchmark. Together, these findings demonstrate why sound evaluation protocols must separate fast development feedback from strictly quarantined held-out benchmarks, while precisely characterizing the underlying task population.

As systems shifted toward autonomous execution, static classification metrics yielded to dynamic task validation. In code synthesis, Chen et al. (2021) introduced the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) metric alongside an unbiased, low-variance estimator to measure execution correctness across multi-sample generation budgets. When automated foundation models were enlisted as evaluators, Zheng et al. (2023) measured the biases of model-based judges, reporting position and verbosity preferences while finding the evidence for self-enhancement bias inconclusive. Benchmark design subsequently evolved from conversational question-answering to interactive, stateful sandbox environments: SWE-bench evaluated software engineering agents against verified test suites on real GitHub issues (Jimenez et al. 2024), and WebArena placed web agents in live, multi-service environments evaluated on final system states (Zhou et al. 2024). Finally, recognizing that single trajectory outcomes conceal non-deterministic variance, Yao et al. (2024) introduced Tau-bench to measure agent consistency across repeated trials, while Kapoor et al. (2024) demonstrated that rigorous agent comparisons require benchmarking cost and resource consumption alongside accuracy along empirical Pareto frontiers.

Chen, Mark, Jerry Tworek, Heewoo Jun, Qiming Yuan, Henrique Ponde de Oliveira Pinto, Jared Kaplan, Harri Edwards, et al. 2021. “Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.03374.
Jimenez, Carlos E., John Yang, Alexander Wettig, Shunyu Yao, Kexin Pei, Ofir Press, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2024. “SWE-Bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Zhou, Shuyan, Frank F. Xu, Hao Zhu, Xuhui Zhou, Robert Lo, Abishek Sridhar, Xianyi Cheng, et al. 2024. “WebArena: A Realistic Web Environment for Building Autonomous Agents.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Further reading.

  • Brown, Cai, and DasGupta, “Interval Estimation for a Binomial Proportion” (Brown et al. 2001): An empirical and theoretical analysis demonstrating why the standard Wald interval breaks down across small samples and boundary probabilities, establishing the superiority of the Wilson score interval for agent evaluation suites.
  • Dwork et al., “The Reusable Holdout” (Dwork et al. 2015): A concise, rigorous treatment explaining how iterative evaluation on a test set degrades its statistical validity through adaptive overfitting, introducing differential privacy mechanisms for sound reuse.
  • Zheng et al., “Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena” (Zheng et al. 2023): A systematic measurement of model-based judges against human preferences, reporting position and verbosity biases, and the starting point for calibrating a judge before it grades acceptance.
  • Kapoor et al., “AI Agents That Matter” (Kapoor et al. 2024): A case study in agent benchmark methodology, advocating for reporting inference costs and resource consumption alongside success rates along empirical Pareto frontiers.
Dwork, Cynthia, Vitaly Feldman, Moritz Hardt, Toniann Pitassi, Omer Reingold, and Aaron Roth. 2015. “The Reusable Holdout: Preserving Validity in Adaptive Data Analysis.” Science 349 (6248): 636–38. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aaa9375.
Zheng, Lianmin, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, Siyuan Zhuang, Hao Wu, Yonghao Zhuang, Zi Lin, et al. 2023. “Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 46595–623.
Kapoor, Sayash, Benedikt Stroebl, Zachary S. Siegel, Nitya Nadgir, and Arvind Narayanan. 2024. “AI Agents That Matter.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2407.01502.

13.9 Exercises

Each problem states its constraints and ends in a decision about what the evidence supports or what to collect next. Givens are hypothetical unless a lighthouse profile supplies them, and every interval is a nominal 95 percent interval with \(\IntervalNormalQuantile=1.96\).

1. New tasks or repeats (Basic). A pilot of the repair agent’s evaluation suite estimates a mean task acceptance probability of 0.6 and a between-task variance \(\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2=0.08\). (a) Derive \(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2\) from these two givens using its definition in equation 1, and find the number of runs per task beyond which the run term of the variance falls below the task term. (b) With a budget of 600 graded runs, and new tasks available at no extra cost, compare the standard error of \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability\) for 600 tasks run once, 200 tasks run 3 times, and 100 tasks run 6 times, and choose a design. (c) Writing and validating a new hermetic fixture in fact costs as much as 10 graded runs. Within the same budget of 600 run-equivalents, find the runs per task \(\PerTaskRunCount\), each with the largest whole task count \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) it affords, that minimizes \(\operatorname{Var}(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability)\). Explain why the best design moved, and state which population the resulting estimate describes.

2. A clean record on the replica (Basic). The remediation agent runs a candidate configuration on an isolated replica for 60 independently sampled incident tasks, and state-delta checks find no wrong change in any of them. From the lighthouse profile, a wrong change that commits costs $12,000, and the human approval that currently gates every change costs $20 of reviewer time. (a) Compute the Wald and Wilson intervals for the wrong-change rate, and state which one a release record can use. (b) Ignoring any later canary check, price the Wilson upper limit as an expected loss per task and compare it with the approval cost. (c) Find how many consecutive clean replica tasks would bring that expected-loss bound below the approval cost. Decide whether the 60 clean runs justify removing human approval, and name the class of evidence that no count of replica runs can supply.

3. Agreement is not accuracy (Basic). Two model judges, A and B, score 200 research-agent reports under the same versioned rubric. Both pass 110 reports, A passes and B fails 10, A fails and B passes 20, and both fail 60. An independent reference assessment of the same reports finds 80 failures, of which B accepted 16, and 120 passing reports, of which B accepted 114. (a) Compute the raw agreement and Cohen’s \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) between the judges. (b) Compute B’s false-acceptance rate \(\Pr(\text{Acc}\mid \ReferenceAssessedFailure)\) with its Wilson interval, and the failure fraction among B’s acceptances, \(\Pr(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\mid\text{Acc})\). (c) The release policy admits a judge as an acceptance grader only if the Wilson upper limit on its false-acceptance rate is at most 10 percent. Decide whether B qualifies, and state what a random audit of B-accepted reports alone could and could not establish.

4. Paired evidence for the offline gate (Intermediate). A repair-agent comparison runs one baseline and one candidate trial on each of 200 independently sampled tasks, with complete verdicts: both accepted on 120 tasks, only the baseline on \(\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount=6\), only the candidate on \(\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount=16\), and neither on 58. The declared tolerance is \(\NoninferiorityTolerance=2\) percentage points. (a) Compute \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference\), the discordant fraction \(\DiscordantPairFraction\), and a normal-approximation paired interval from the variance \((\DiscordantPairFraction-\PairedAcceptanceDifference^2)/\EvaluationSampleCount\). Decide whether the candidate is noninferior and whether it is superior. (b) Compute each configuration’s separate Wilson interval, and show what a team that required non-overlapping intervals would have decided. (c) Compute the ratio of independent-arm variance to paired variance at these acceptance rates, and the number of tasks per independent arm that would match the paired precision. (d) Compute the exact two-sided McNemar value. Explain how it can sit just above 0.05 while the paired interval excludes zero, and state which result the declared gate uses.

5. Choose the generator, not the cap (Intermediate). The repair agent selects sequentially, stopping at the first candidate that passes an exact checker costing \(\CandidateCheckingCost=\$0.04\) per candidate. Generator A passes with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.3\) at \(\CandidateGenerationCost=\$0.05\) per candidate; generator B passes with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.5\) at \(\CandidateGenerationCost=\$0.12\). (a) Compute \(\SequentialCostPerAcceptedTask\) for each generator. (b) The task deadline allows at most 4 sequential candidates, and the contract requires availability \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k\ge 0.90\) within that cap. Determine which generator meets it, and the smallest cap generator A would need. (c) A teammate proposes generator A with all candidates generated and checked in parallel at the cap from (b) so that the deadline holds. Compute \(\BatchCostPerAcceptedTask\) for that design and compare it with generator B under sequential selection. (d) The team replaces the checker with the full regression suite at \(\CandidateCheckingCost=\$0.20\). Recompute both sequential costs, find the checking cost at which the two generators tie, and state the design rule the crossover illustrates.

6. Second lighthouse: the support agent’s consistency (Intermediate). The support agent answers a refund-policy question correctly on 85 percent of single runs, and the same customer asks it in 5 separate sessions. A wrong answer that a customer has read cannot be retried, and a human handoff costs $4 per session. (a) Compute pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) over the 5 sessions, the single-run rate that would hold 90 percent consistency, and the factor by which the single-run failure rate must fall. For contrast, compute pass@\(3\) at the same single-run rate, the quantity a repair agent with a checker in the loop could use. (b) A policy-grounded model judge now screens each answer before delivery, with \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate=0.20\) and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate=0.05\), and every rejected answer goes to a human handoff. Per session, compute the probability that a wrong answer reaches the customer, the handoff probability, and the expected handoff cost; then compute the probability that all 5 sessions deliver no wrong answer. (c) Find the largest \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) that holds 90 percent over the 5 sessions, and the expected handoff cost per session at that rate. (d) Contrast the constraint that binds this agent’s evaluation claim with the one that binds the repair agent’s, and state which grader measurement the support agent’s release record must therefore carry.

What’s Next: From offline evidence to live operation
An offline comparison ends with a verdict about a configuration on a sampled task population, and for candidate_v2 that verdict is inconclusive. It cannot say why a particular trial failed, and it says nothing about what the configuration does once it acts on live work with real authority. Operating Agents follows the connection-pool repair past this point: traces bind each model call, tool execution, and check to its trial, while verdict-aware retention keeps rare failures within storage and privacy budgets. Controlled postmortems then test an explanation by intervening on the recorded trajectory, and staged releases bound what an undetected regression can do before a declared gate decides.
